I never miss a Sunday service if I can help it, and I always, always help out with the Sunday potlucks. I usually bring my green bean casserole, the one with the little crispy onions on top, you know? Everybody just loves it and expects it. But lately... well, I came because I just keep doing these odd things while I'm there. Just real strange things that I can't quite make sense of. \n\nLike just this past Sunday, we were all lined up for the food, and I was standing right there by the potluck table. I had the big metal serving spoon in my hand, getting ready to serve, and I just... I lifted it up, and then I just held it up in the air. And I didn't move. I don't know for how long I was like that, but I just stood there completely frozen like a statue with that heavy spoon up in the air. Folks were waiting right behind me with their empty paper plates, just staring at me, wondering what on earth I was doing. \n\nAnd it wasn't just that one time, either. A couple weeks before that, I was supposed to be setting out the paper goods. We use those thick white square napkins, the nice ones, because they hold up better with the barbecue sauce. Well, I was standing at the end of the table, and I started folding this one stack of napkins. And I just kept folding the exact same stack over and over again. Just creasing them and folding them, the same little pile, over and over. There was a whole line of people waiting on me to hand them one so they could go sit down and eat, and I just couldn't stop folding that same stack. I don't know why I do it, my hands just get stuck doing the same thing, or doing nothing at all. It's just so terribly embarrassing, Doctor, having all those nice people waiting on me and looking at me like I've lost my senses...\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, are you fully aware of what you're doing, or does it feel like you're not completely in control of your actions?\n\nPatient: Well, that's just the strangest part of the whole thing, Doctor. I mean, I'm right there in the fellowship hall, I can hear Pastor Davis talking to Mrs. Higgins about her prize-winning tomatoes, I can smell the fried chicken from the bucket that the Miller family always brings from the deli down the street, and I can see the line of folks waiting on me. So I know I'm there. I know exactly what's happening around me. \n\nBut as for being in control... well, it's just like my body decides to take a little vacation without my permission. Like with that serving spoon, I knew my arm was up in the air, I could feel the cold metal of the handle pressing into my palm, and I knew I ought to just lower it down and scoop up some of that green bean casserole. I kept telling myself, 'Martha, put the spoon down, the Johnson boy is hungry and waiting.' But my arm... it just wouldn't listen. It was like it was made of lead, or like somebody else had taken over the strings, like I was one of those marionette puppets you see on the television programs. \n\nAnd with the napkins, oh, it was the same sort of feeling. I knew I had already folded that little stack. I could see the creases were perfectly sharp, just the way my mother taught me to do them when I was a little girl helping set the table for Sunday dinner. But my hands just kept on going, folding and pressing, folding and pressing. I wanted to stop, I really did, especially when I saw the line backing up all the way to the double doors, but it was like my hands had a mind of their very own. It's just... it's terribly unsettling, Doctor, to feel like you're a passenger in your own body, just watching yourself do these peculiar things and not being able to put a stop to it. I've never felt anything quite like it in all my seventy-one years, and I just don't understand what's gone wrong with my wiring, so to speak.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever feel confused, detached, or as if you're not fully aware of what's happening around you?\n\nPatient: No, no, not confused at all. That's what I was trying to say just a minute ago, Doctor. My mind is as sharp as a tack when it happens. I don't feel far away or detached or anything like that. \n\nWhy, just when I had that spoon up in the air, I wasn't off in the clouds. I was looking right at little Timmy Johnson—he's the middle boy, you know, the one who just started the third grade with Mrs. Gable over at the county school. I could see this little yellow mustard stain on his Sunday shirt, right on the collar. And I remember thinking to myself, clear as a bell, 'Oh, his poor mother is going to have a time getting that out,' because mustard is just the devil to wash out of white cotton. You have to soak it in vinegar and baking soda before you even think about putting it in the washing machine, my own mother taught me that back when we had to scrub everything by hand on a washboard. \n\nSo you see, I'm taking every little thing in. I hear the clinking of the ice in the sweet tea glasses, I see the ceiling fans turning—the one over by the piano always has a little wobble to it, it goes tick-tick-tick, and I can hear it plain as day. I'm right there in the room. I'm not detached one bit. It's just my body playing these cruel tricks on me, but my mind... well, my mind is sitting right there in the front row, watching the whole embarrassing show unfold. I don't lose track of anything, not for a single second. It would almost be a blessing if I was confused, honestly, because then maybe I wouldn't feel so awfully foolish standing there like a block of wood while everybody stares. But no, I know exactly what's going on the whole entire time.\n\nDoctor: Martha, outside of these episodes at church, have you noticed any other times when part of your body moves on its own or feels stiff or tight without you meaning to?\n\nPatient: Well now, outside of the church... let me just think on that for a second, Doctor. You see, I spend most of my time just keeping house. I live over on Elm Street in this little blue house with a wrap-around porch—my late husband Arthur, God rest his soul, he bought it for us way back in nineteen and seventy-two. I do all my own cleaning, I sweep that porch every single morning, rain or shine, and I haven't noticed my arms or legs locking up on me while I'm pushing the broom. And when I'm sitting in my floral recliner in the afternoons watching my stories—I always watch my programs after lunch, I have for years and years—I'm just resting, so I suppose I wouldn't notice much stiffness then anyway. \n\nBut no, like I was trying to explain to you before, that's exactly the whole reason I finally agreed to come in and see a doctor today. It's because I just keep doing these odd things specifically when I'm at the church. If it happened at home, well, the only one to see me looking foolish would be my old tabby cat, Barnaby, and he wouldn't mind one bit. But it's happening right there at the fellowship hall, just like I said, where I'll be standing by the potluck table holding that heavy serving spoon up in the air and just not moving a single muscle. Or I'll get entirely stuck folding that exact same stack of napkins over and over and over again while all those nice folks are just standing in line waiting on me to hand them one. It's just those peculiar things at the church that brought me here, because I just can't make heads or tails of why my body only decides to act up and freeze on me when I'm trying to serve my neighbors.\n\nDoctor: Martha, have you ever noticed any stiffness, freezing, or movements that you couldn’t control when you’re doing something else—maybe when you’re upset, concentrating, or not thinking about what your body is doing?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor... I don't mean to sound like a broken record, bless your heart, but like I was just saying, I really haven't noticed it any other time at all. Even if I'm terribly upset—like last month when my neighbor's dog dug up my prize petunias, I was just beside myself, but my body didn't freeze up or do anything strange then. And when I'm concentrating real hard on my knitting or reading my Bible, my hands work perfectly fine. \n\nIt really is just exactly what I was trying to tell you a moment ago. The whole reason I finally made this appointment and came in today is specifically because at church, I just keep doing these odd things. It's only ever there. I'll be standing right by the potluck table, holding the serving spoon straight up in the air and just... not moving. Not an inch. Just frozen there like a statue while the food gets cold. Or I'll be at the end of the table, and I'll be folding the exact same stack of napkins over and over and over again, just smoothing down the edges, while all those nice folks are standing there in a line waiting on me to hand them one so they can eat. \n\nThat's the whole reason I'm sitting in this chair right now, Doctor, just those peculiar things at the fellowship hall. I just can't figure out why my body only decides to act up when I'm trying to help out with the church supper, but I assure you, it doesn't happen when I'm just going about my normal business or getting upset about something else.\n\nDoctor: Martha, do these episodes only happen when you're in front of a group of people, like at church, or have you ever noticed them happening when you're alone doing something, even at home?\n\nPatient: Well now, Doctor, I know you're just trying to get to the bottom of this, and I appreciate that, I really do. But honestly, it's just like I've been trying to tell you. When I'm all by my lonesome at the house, I am as right as rain. \n\nI spend a good deal of my time alone these days, you know, since my Arthur passed on. It's mostly just me and Barnaby. And I do plenty of chores where, if my body was going to play a trick on me and freeze up, you'd think it would happen then! Why, just yesterday morning I was putting up my tomatoes. I grow those Big Boy tomatoes out by the back fence, and I have to stand over that hot stove boiling the Mason jars to sterilize them. My mother always told me, she said, 'Martha, if you don't boil those jars a full ten minutes, you'll spoil the whole batch and make somebody sick.' So I'm standing there, lifting those heavy glass jars out of the boiling water with my metal tongs... now, if my arm was going to lock up and get stuck in the air, you'd surely think it would happen right then and there with boiling hot water over my clean linoleum floor! \n\nBut it never does. Not once. I move just as smooth as you please when I'm by myself. I can cook a whole Sunday dinner, I can scrub the baseboards, I can hang all my sheets out on the clothesline in the backyard, and my hands and arms do exactly what I tell them to do. It really is only when I'm standing up there in front of the whole congregation at the fellowship hall. I don't know if it's all those eyes on me or what, because I've been serving at that church for forty years and never had a lick of trouble until recently. But I can tell you for an absolute fact, it never happens when I'm just puttering around my own house by myself.\n\nDoctor: Martha, during these episodes at church, do you ever feel confused about where you are, or have any strange sensations or thoughts that something isn't quite real?\n\nPatient: Well, no, Doctor, not at all. I know exactly where I am the whole entire time. I mean, I've been going to First Baptist since... oh, it must be nineteen and sixty-eight. Arthur and I joined right after we got married. We used to sit in the fourth pew on the left side, right under that beautiful stained glass window with the dove on it. I know every inch of that church. I know the fellowship hall, I know the kitchen, I know the ladies' parlor where we have our quilting circle on Tuesdays. \n\nSo when I'm standing there by the potluck table with the serving spoon stuck up in the air, or when I'm folding those napkins over and over, I am fully aware that I am standing in the fellowship hall of First Baptist Church. I can see Reverend Thomas over by the punch bowl, I can hear Mrs. Higgins complaining about the air conditioning being too high—she always complains it's too drafty in there, even in August—and I can smell the fried chicken and the green bean casserole. It all feels perfectly real to me. It's just my body that's acting strange, not my mind. My mind is just as sharp as a tack, taking it all in, wondering why in heaven's name my arm won't just move and put the spoon down into the mashed potatoes. I'm just trapped there, looking at everyone, knowing exactly what's going on around me, but I just can't make myself move. So no, I don't get confused or feel like things aren't real, not one bit.\n\nDoctor: Martha, have you ever noticed any stiffness, freezing, or movements that you couldn’t control when you’re doing something else—maybe when you’re upset, concentrating, or not thinking about what your body is doing?\n\nPatient: Well now, Doctor, I... I feel like we might be going in circles a little bit here, bless your heart. Like I was just saying a few minutes ago, I really haven't noticed anything like that at all outside of the church hall. I know you have to ask all your questions, and I don't mind answering them, but it's exactly like I told you. Not when I'm upset, and not when I'm concentrating. \n\nWhy, just the other evening, I was sitting in my good armchair—the blue one Arthur bought me for our twenty-fifth anniversary back in ninety-two, it's got the nicest floral pattern on it—and I was concentrating as hard as a person can on balancing my checkbook. The electric bill was higher than a cat's back this month, just outrageous, and I was so frustrated trying to make the numbers match up in my little ledger. I must have sat there for a solid hour, squinting at those little boxes with my reading glasses, writing and erasing, writing and erasing. \n\nAnd my hands? They did exactly what they were supposed to do. No stiffness, no freezing up like a statue, no getting stuck holding my pencil in the air. And I certainly wasn't thinking about what my body was doing, I was just thinking about how on earth the power company thinks a widow woman on a fixed income can afford such rates! So, no, it's just like I said before. It's only ever at the church supper when I'm standing there with the food. Never any other time, no matter how hard I'm concentrating or how upset I get.\n\nDoctor: Martha, during these episodes, do you ever find yourself unable to remember what happened afterward, or do you remember everything clearly?\n\nPatient: Well, no, Doctor, I remember every single second of it. Like I was saying just a moment ago, my mind is completely clear the whole time. I don't lose any time or forget what happened, not a bit of it. \n\nI can tell you exactly who came up to the table while I was stuck there holding that spoon. Last Sunday, it was Betty Miller. She came right up with her little paper plate, and she was wearing that awful yellow dress with the big daisies on it—I don't know who told her yellow was her color, but it certainly is not, especially with her complexion. Anyway, she stood there waiting for me to serve her the potato salad, and I just couldn't move. I remember looking right at her, seeing the confusion on her face. She said, 'Martha, dear, are you alright?' And I remember thinking, 'Of course I'm alright, Betty, just take the spoon from me!' But I couldn't say it. \n\nAnd then Reverend Thomas came over, and I remember him putting his hand on my shoulder—he has such heavy hands, you know, he means well, but he just claps you on the shoulder like you're one of the men. And I remember the smell of his aftershave, it's that Old Spice, the exact same kind Arthur used to wear. It always makes me a little teary when I smell it, to be honest. But I remember all of that happening while I was just frozen there. \n\nAnd afterwards, when I finally could move again and I had to sit down in one of those folding chairs—the metal ones that are so cold on your back—I remembered every little detail of what had just happened. I sat there replaying it in my head, feeling so embarrassed that everyone saw me like that. So no, I don't forget a single thing. It's all locked up here in my head, clear as a bell, from the moment I freeze up to the moment I can finally put my arm down again.\n\nDoctor: Martha, have you ever noticed any stiffness, freezing, or movements that you couldn’t control when you’re doing something else—maybe when you’re upset, concentrating, or not thinking about what your body is doing?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor, like I... I feel like we might be getting our wires crossed again, bless your heart. We just talked about this, didn't we? I know you have your list of questions, and I'm happy to answer them, I really am. But it's just like I was explaining before. I don't have any trouble when I'm at home, or when I'm upset, or when I'm trying to balance that awful checkbook of mine. \n\nThe only time I have these problems, the whole reason I even made this appointment and came all the way down here to see you—which is quite a trip for me these days, let me tell you, what with the traffic on Route 9 getting worse every single year—is because of what happens at the church. \n\nIt's just those odd things I keep doing at the First Baptist fellowship hall. Like I said, I'll just be standing there by the potluck table, holding the serving spoon straight up in the air, and I just stop moving. Just frozen like a statue, while poor Betty Miller or whoever else is standing there waiting for their potato salad. Or I'll be at the end of the table, and I'll start folding the same stack of paper napkins over and over and over again. I'll just keep creasing them and folding them, and people are waiting in line behind me, and I know they're waiting, but I just keep doing it. It's so terribly embarrassing, Doctor, having everyone looking at me while I'm doing these strange things and holding up the supper line. But that's the only place it happens. Never when I'm just sitting at home concentrating on something else. Only ever at the church.\n\nDoctor: Martha, have you ever noticed any stiffness, freezing, or movements that you couldn’t control when you’re upset, concentrating, or not thinking about what your body is doing—say, during moments of strong emotion or when you're distracted?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... I just don't know how else to say this, I really don't. We've gone over this exact same thing three times now, haven't we? I know you're just doing your job, and I'm trying my very best to be helpful, but my answer hasn't changed since the first time you asked me. \n\nNo, I don't have any freezing or stiffness or anything like that when I'm upset or distracted or concentrating. I don't get stuck like a statue when I'm watching my stories on the television, and I don't start folding napkins when I'm on the phone with my sister in Omaha—and believe me, those calls can get very emotional, she's always going on about her daughter's husband and his terrible business ideas, it's enough to make anyone upset. But my body works just fine then. \n\nIt is only, and I mean *only*, at the First Baptist fellowship hall. Just like I told you. Not when I'm paying bills, not when I'm upset, not when I'm distracted. I'm sorry if I sound a bit sharp, Doctor, I really am, I was raised better than to snap at a professional. But I feel like a broken record, just saying the same thing over and over again about the church suppers and the potato salad and the napkins. That's the only time it happens, I promise you.\n\nDoctor: Martha, have you ever noticed any stiffness, freezing, or movements that you couldn’t control when you’re upset, concentrating, or not thinking about what your body is doing—say, during moments of strong emotion or when you're distracted?\n\nPatient: Doctor, are you... are you feeling quite alright today? Because I do believe you just asked me that exact same question. Word for word, even! I'm starting to wonder if you're testing my memory instead of my nerves. I don't mean to be disrespectful, truly I don't, but my goodness. \n\nMy late husband Arthur, God rest his soul, he used to ask me the same thing three or four times when his hearing started to go in his sixties. I'd tell him, 'Arthur, the pot roast is in the oven,' and five minutes later he'd wander into the kitchen and say, 'Martha, where's the pot roast?' But you're a young doctor with a perfectly good set of ears. \n\nSo I will tell you again, just as plain as I can manage. No. I don't get stiff or freeze up when I'm emotional, and I don't do it when I'm concentrating on my knitting—which takes a lot of concentration these days, let me tell you, because my arthritis makes my knuckles swell up so terribly, especially when the weather turns damp like it has been all week. And I don't do it when I'm distracted. My body works exactly the way it's supposed to every other minute of the day. \n\nThe only place on God's green earth that I turn into a statue or start folding those napkins to death is that fellowship hall at the First Baptist Church. Just there. I really don't know why we keep going in circles about this, Doctor, I really don't.\n\nDoctor: Martha, can you think of any time—maybe when you were startled, angry, or distracted—when your body suddenly froze or moved on its own, even just for a second, outside of the church setting?\n\nPatient: Doctor... I just... I honestly don't know what to do with you at this point. I really don't. Are you writing any of this down in that folder of yours? Because I feel like I am speaking into a tin can with the string cut. \n\nYou asked about being startled or angry. Well, let me tell you, just this past Tuesday, that little Henderson boy from three houses down—the one whose mother never makes him tuck his shirt in, bless her heart, she tries but she works two jobs—he set off a whole string of those loud firecrackers right next to my prized hydrangeas. Sounded like a cannon going off! Scared the living daylights out of me, it really did. I was standing right there on the porch holding my heavy metal watering can. And did I freeze up like a statue? Did my arm get stuck? No, it certainly did not. I marched right down those steps, clear across the lawn, and gave him a piece of my mind, waving that watering can around the whole time. \n\nAnd as for being angry, Lord have mercy, when the manager at the grocery store tried to charge me a dollar forty-nine for my canned peaches last week when the sign clearly said ninety-nine cents, I was fit to be tied. But my hands worked just fine to point right at that receipt, and I didn't freeze for a single second while I waited for him to fix it. \n\nSo no, Doctor. Not for a second. Not when I am mad, not when I jump out of my skin, not when I am doing a crossword puzzle. Never outside of that church building. I truly do not know how many different ways I can explain the exact same thing to you. I am starting to think we are going to be sitting here until the streetlights come on just talking about this one thing!\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. You see, I spend most of my time just keeping house. I live over on Elm Street in this little blue house with a wrap-around porch—my late husband Arthur, God rest his soul, he bought it for us way back in nineteen and seventy-two. I do all my own cleaning, I sweep that porch every single morning, rain or shine, and I haven't noticed my arms or legs locking up on me while I'm pushing the broom. And when I'm sitting in my floral recliner in the afternoons watching my stories—I always watch my programs after lunch, I have for years and years—I'm just resting, so I suppose I wouldn't notice much stiffness then anyway. \n\nBut no, like I was trying to explain to you before, that's exactly the whole reason I finally agreed to come in and see a doctor today. It's because I just keep doing these odd things specifically when I'm at the church. If it happened at home, well, the only one to see me looking foolish would be my old tabby cat, Barnaby, and he wouldn't mind one bit. But it's happening right there at the fellowship hall, just like I said, where I'll be standing by the potluck table holding that heavy serving spoon up in the air and just not moving a single muscle. Or I'll get entirely stuck folding that exact same stack of napkins over and over and over again while all those nice folks are just standing in line waiting on me to hand them one. It's just those peculiar things at the church that brought me here, because I just can't make heads or tails of why my body only decides to act up and freeze on me when I'm trying to serve my neighbors.\n\nDoctor: Martha, have you ever noticed any stiffness, freezing, or movements that you couldn’t control when you’re doing something else—maybe when you’re upset, concentrating, or not thinking about what your body is doing?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor... I don't mean to sound like a broken record, bless your heart, but like I was just saying, I really haven't noticed it any other time at all. Even if I'm terribly upset—like last month when my neighbor's dog dug up my prize petunias, I was just beside myself, but my body didn't freeze up or do anything strange then. And when I'm concentrating real hard on my knitting or reading my Bible, my hands work perfectly fine. \n\nIt really is just exactly what I was trying to tell you a moment ago. The whole reason I finally made this appointment and came in today is specifically because at church, I just keep doing these odd things. It's only ever there. I'll be standing right by the potluck table, holding the serving spoon straight up in the air and just... not moving. Not an inch. Just frozen there like a statue while the food gets cold. Or I'll be at the end of the table, and I'll be folding the exact same stack of napkins over and over and over again, just smoothing down the edges, while all those nice folks are standing there in a line waiting on me to hand them one so they can eat. \n\nThat's the whole reason I'm sitting in this chair right now, Doctor, just those peculiar things at the fellowship hall. I just can't figure out why my body only decides to act up when I'm trying to help out with the church supper, but I assure you, it doesn't happen when I'm just going about my normal business or getting upset about something else.\n\nDoctor: Martha, do these episodes only happen when you're in front of a group of people, like at church, or have you ever noticed them happening when you're alone doing something, even at home?\n\nPatient: Well now, Doctor, I know you're just trying to get to the bottom of this, and I appreciate that, I really do. But honestly, it's just like I've been trying to tell you. When I'm all by my lonesome at the house, I am as right as rain. \n\nI spend a good deal of my time alone these days, you know, since my Arthur passed on. It's mostly just me and Barnaby. And I do plenty of chores where, if my body was going to play a trick on me and freeze up, you'd think it would happen then! Why, just yesterday morning I was putting up my tomatoes. I grow those Big Boy tomatoes out by the back fence, and I have to stand over that hot stove boiling the Mason jars to sterilize them. My mother always told me, she said, 'Martha, if you don't boil those jars a full ten minutes, you'll spoil the whole batch and make somebody sick.' So I'm standing there, lifting those heavy glass jars out of the boiling water with my metal tongs... now, if my arm was going to lock up and get stuck in the air, you'd surely think it would happen right then and there with boiling hot water over my clean linoleum floor! \n\nBut it never does. Not once. I move just as smooth as you please when I'm by myself. I can cook a whole Sunday dinner, I can scrub the baseboards, I can hang all my sheets out on the clothesline in the backyard, and my hands and arms do exactly what I tell them to do. It really is only when I'm standing up there in front of the whole congregation at the fellowship hall. I don't know if it's all those eyes on me or what, because I've been serving at that church for forty years and never had a lick of trouble until recently. But I can tell you for an absolute fact, it never happens when I'm just puttering around my own house by myself.\n\nDoctor: Martha, during these episodes at church, do you ever feel confused about where you are, or have any strange sensations or thoughts that something isn't quite real?\n\nPatient: Well, no, Doctor, not at all. I know exactly where I am the whole entire time. I mean, I've been going to First Baptist since... oh, it must be nineteen and sixty-eight. Arthur and I joined right after we got married. We used to sit in the fourth pew on the left side, right under that beautiful stained glass window with the dove on it. I know every inch of that church. I know the fellowship hall, I know the kitchen, I know the ladies' parlor where we have our quilting circle on Tuesdays. \n\nSo when I'm standing there by the potluck table with the serving spoon stuck up in the air, or when I'm folding those napkins over and over, I am fully aware that I am standing in the fellowship hall of First Baptist Church. I can see Reverend Thomas over by the punch bowl, I can hear Mrs. Higgins complaining about the air conditioning being too high—she always complains it's too drafty in there, even in August—and I can smell the fried chicken and the green bean casserole. It all feels perfectly real to me. It's just my body that's acting strange, not my mind. My mind is just as sharp as a tack, taking it all in, wondering why in heaven's name my arm won't just move and put the spoon down into the mashed potatoes. I'm just trapped there, looking at everyone, knowing exactly what's going on around me, but I just can't make myself move. So no, I don't get confused or feel like things aren't real, not one bit.\n\nDoctor: Martha, have you ever noticed any stiffness, freezing, or movements that you couldn’t control when you’re doing something else—maybe when you’re upset, concentrating, or not thinking about what your body is doing?\n\nPatient: Well now, Doctor, I... I feel like we might be going in circles a little bit here, bless your heart. Like I was just saying a few minutes ago, I really haven't noticed anything like that at all outside of the church hall. I know you have to ask all your questions, and I don't mind answering them, but it's exactly like I told you. Not when I'm upset, and not when I'm concentrating. \n\nWhy, just the other evening, I was sitting in my good armchair—the blue one Arthur bought me for our twenty-fifth anniversary back in ninety-two, it's got the nicest floral pattern on it—and I was concentrating as hard as a person can on balancing my checkbook. The electric bill was higher than a cat's back this month, just outrageous, and I was so frustrated trying to make the numbers match up in my little ledger. I must have sat there for a solid hour, squinting at those little boxes with my reading glasses, writing and erasing, writing and erasing. \n\nAnd my hands? They did exactly what they were supposed to do. No stiffness, no freezing up like a statue, no getting stuck holding my pencil in the air. And I certainly wasn't thinking about what my body was doing, I was just thinking about how on earth the power company thinks a widow woman on a fixed income can afford such rates! So, no, it's just like I said before. It's only ever at the church supper when I'm standing there with the food. Never any other time, no matter how hard I'm concentrating or how upset I get.\n\nDoctor: Martha, during these episodes, do you ever find yourself unable to remember what happened afterward, or do you remember everything clearly?\n\nPatient: Well, no, Doctor, I remember every single second of it. Like I was saying just a moment ago, my mind is completely clear the whole time. I don't lose any time or forget what happened, not a bit of it. \n\nI can tell you exactly who came up to the table while I was stuck there holding that spoon. Last Sunday, it was Betty Miller. She came right up with her little paper plate, and she was wearing that awful yellow dress with the big daisies on it—I don't know who told her yellow was her color, but it certainly is not, especially with her complexion. Anyway, she stood there waiting for me to serve her the potato salad, and I just couldn't move. I remember looking right at her, seeing the confusion on her face. She said, 'Martha, dear, are you alright?' And I remember thinking, 'Of course I'm alright, Betty, just take the spoon from me!' But I couldn't say it. \n\nAnd then Reverend Thomas came over, and I remember him putting his hand on my shoulder—he has such heavy hands, you know, he means well, but he just claps you on the shoulder like you're one of the men. And I remember the smell of his aftershave, it's that Old Spice, the exact same kind Arthur used to wear. It always makes me a little teary when I smell it, to be honest. But I remember all of that happening while I was just frozen there. \n\nAnd afterwards, when I finally could move again and I had to sit down in one of those folding chairs—the metal ones that are so cold on your back—I remembered every little detail of what had just happened. I sat there replaying it in my head, feeling so embarrassed that everyone saw me like that. So no, I don't forget a single thing. It's all locked up here in my head, clear as a bell, from the moment I freeze up to the moment I can finally put my arm down again.\n\nDoctor: Martha, have you ever noticed any stiffness, freezing, or movements that you couldn’t control when you’re doing something else—maybe when you’re upset, concentrating, or not thinking about what your body is doing?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor, like I... I feel like we might be getting our wires crossed again, bless your heart. We just talked about this, didn't we? I know you have your list of questions, and I'm happy to answer them, I really am. But it's just like I was explaining before. I don't have any trouble when I'm at home, or when I'm upset, or when I'm trying to balance that awful checkbook of mine. \n\nThe only time I have these problems, the whole reason I even made this appointment and came all the way down here to see you—which is quite a trip for me these days, let me tell you, what with the traffic on Route 9 getting worse every single year—is because of what happens at the church. \n\nIt's just those odd things I keep doing at the First Baptist fellowship hall. Like I said, I'll just be standing there by the potluck table, holding the serving spoon straight up in the air, and I just stop moving. Just frozen like a statue, while poor Betty Miller or whoever else is standing there waiting for their potato salad. Or I'll be at the end of the table, and I'll start folding the same stack of paper napkins over and over and over again. I'll just keep creasing them and folding them, and people are waiting in line behind me, and I know they're waiting, but I just keep doing it. It's so terribly embarrassing, Doctor, having everyone looking at me while I'm doing these strange things and holding up the supper line. But that's the only place it happens. Never when I'm just sitting at home concentrating on something else. Only ever at the church.\n\nDoctor: Martha, have you ever noticed any stiffness, freezing, or movements that you couldn’t control when you’re upset, concentrating, or not thinking about what your body is doing—say, during moments of strong emotion or when you're distracted?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... I just don't know how else to say this, I really don't. We've gone over this exact same thing three times now, haven't we? I know you're just doing your job, and I'm trying my very best to be helpful, but my answer hasn't changed since the first time you asked me. \n\nNo, I don't have any freezing or stiffness or anything like that when I'm upset or distracted or concentrating. I don't get stuck like a statue when I'm watching my stories on the television, and I don't start folding napkins when I'm on the phone with my sister in Omaha—and believe me, those calls can get very emotional, she's always going on about her daughter's husband and his terrible business ideas, it's enough to make anyone upset. But my body works just fine then. \n\nIt is only, and I mean *only*, at the First Baptist fellowship hall. Just like I told you. Not when I'm paying bills, not when I'm upset, not when I'm distracted. I'm sorry if I sound a bit sharp, Doctor, I really am, I was raised better than to snap at a professional. But I feel like a broken record, just saying the same thing over and over again about the church suppers and the potato salad and the napkins. That's the only time it happens, I promise you.\n\nDoctor: Martha, have you ever noticed any stiffness, freezing, or movements that you couldn’t control when you’re upset, concentrating, or not thinking about what your body is doing—say, during moments of strong emotion or when you're distracted?\n\nPatient: Doctor, are you... are you feeling quite alright today? Because I do believe you just asked me that exact same question. Word for word, even! I'm starting to wonder if you're testing my memory instead of my nerves. I don't mean to be disrespectful, truly I don't, but my goodness. \n\nMy late husband Arthur, God rest his soul, he used to ask me the same thing three or four times when his hearing started to go in his sixties. I'd tell him, 'Arthur, the pot roast is in the oven,' and five minutes later he'd wander into the kitchen and say, 'Martha, where's the pot roast?' But you're a young doctor with a perfectly good set of ears. \n\nSo I will tell you again, just as plain as I can manage. No. I don't get stiff or freeze up when I'm emotional, and I don't do it when I'm concentrating on my knitting—which takes a lot of concentration these days, let me tell you, because my arthritis makes my knuckles swell up so terribly, especially when the weather turns damp like it has been all week. And I don't do it when I'm distracted. My body works exactly the way it's supposed to every other minute of the day. \n\nThe only place on God's green earth that I turn into a statue or start folding those napkins to death is that fellowship hall at the First Baptist Church. Just there. I really don't know why we keep going in circles about this, Doctor, I really don't.\n\nDoctor: Martha, can you think of any time—maybe when you were startled, angry, or distracted—when your body suddenly froze or moved on its own, even just for a second, outside of the church setting?\n\nPatient: Doctor... I just... I honestly don't know what to do with you at this point. I really don't. Are you writing any of this down in that folder of yours? Because I feel like I am speaking into a tin can with the string cut. \n\nYou asked about being startled or angry. Well, let me tell you, just this past Tuesday, that little Henderson boy from three houses down—the one whose mother never makes him tuck his shirt in, bless her heart, she tries but she works two jobs—he set off a whole string of those loud firecrackers right next to my prized hydrangeas. Sounded like a cannon going off! Scared the living daylights out of me, it really did. I was standing right there on the porch holding my heavy metal watering can. And did I freeze up like a statue? Did my arm get stuck? No, it certainly did not. I marched right down those steps, clear across the lawn, and gave him a piece of my mind, waving that watering can around the whole time. \n\nAnd as for being angry, Lord have mercy, when the manager at the grocery store tried to charge me a dollar forty-nine for my canned peaches last week when the sign clearly said ninety-nine cents, I was fit to be tied. But my hands worked just fine to point right at that receipt, and I didn't freeze for a single second while I waited for him to fix it. \n\nSo no, Doctor. Not for a second. Not when I am mad, not when I jump out of my skin, not when I am doing a crossword puzzle. Never outside of that church building. I truly do not know how many different ways I can explain the exact same thing to you. I am starting to think we are going to be sitting here until the streetlights come on just talking about this one thing!"
